Furthermore, the delivery technologies including adeno-associated viral vectors, and non-viral methods are critically evaluated to assess their respective advantages, limitations, and suitability for different testicular cell types. The review also addresses critical safety considerations, such as off-target effects, germline transmission risks, and epigenetic consequences. Finally, we discuss the translational roadmap toward clinical application, and how the obstacles including delivery optimization, long-term safety validation, and the regulatory pathways to affect the transition from preclinical proof-of-concept to first-in-human trials.

Luteolin inhibits prostate cancer stem cells through disrupting the lactate/H3K18la/β-catenin/LDHA feedback loop.
Zhang Q, Ren X, Cao W, …, Zhu M
▾

Abstract

Prostate cancer is one of the most common malignant tumors in men, with prostate cancer stem cells (PCSCs) playing an important role in its initiation and progression. Accumulating evidence has demonstrated that lactate and lactate-mediated histone lactylation are crucial regulators of proliferation, metastasis, immune escape and drug resistance in various cancer. However, their functional roles and underlying mechanisms in PCSCs remain largely elusive. In the present study, we found that lactate levels were significantly elevated in PCSC microenvironment, which effectively induced H3K18 lactylation (H3K18la). Collectively, our findings uncover a novel lactate-driven regulatory axis in PCSCs and provide new insights into the development of intervention strategies in prostate cancer.

Handb Clin Neurol · 2026-10-15
Central epigenetic mechanisms of overeating and thermal regulation.
Meiri N, Weller A
▾

Abstract

This chapter discusses epigenetic mechanisms underlying the central control of homeostasis, focusing on energy intake (and expenditure) and on body temperature. Research, mostly performed in rodents, shows a wide range of epigenetic mechanisms involved in regulation of feeding and body weight, which, when challenged, result in overeating and obesity in vulnerable individuals. Many studies found prominent sex differences and intergenerational transfer of overweight and obesity, with concomitant transfer of epigenetic marks. The anterior hypothalamic preoptic area has a pivotal role in homeostasis and energy expenditure, maintaining body temperature via a central neural network, enabling the body to adapt to fluctuations in environmental temperature. This thermoregulatory network establishes a dynamic setpoint for temperature control, which is genetically determined. It is further refined during critical periods of early prenatal and postnatal development with continuous adaptation throughout life. These adaptations may be heritable, and there is evidence supporting their regulation and maintenance by several layers of epigenetic mechanisms. Further research is needed to unravel epigenetic mechanisms underlying regulation of eating, body weight, energy expenditure, and body temperature, as well as for sex differences and intergenerational transfer of these essential regulatory processes.

Evol Appl · 2026-10-14
Assessing Future Maladaptation Risk in the Keystone Species Populus tremuloides.
Zacharias M, Goessen R, Wehenkel C, …, Porth I
▾

Abstract

Understanding the adaptive potential of widespread trees is critical for predicting species resilience under climate change and informing their conservation strategies. We assessed the genomic offset in Populus tremuloides Michx. (aspen) using 10,937 genotyping-by-sequencing derived, high-quality SNPs from 891 individuals sampled across the species' range, from Mexico to both the eastern and western regions of North America. Gradient forest models were applied using multiple SNP datasets and climate scenarios, with the genomic offset standardized by aspen population genetic clusters and ecological regions to identify future habitat suitability (recipient importance) and potential sources of reproductive material (donor importance) for assisted migration. Moisture-related variables exerted consistently stronger selective pressure on genetic variation than temperature, reflecting the riparian ecology of aspen. Populations from Mexico and the southwestern U.S.A. exhibited the highest offsets, suggesting elevated vulnerability despite apparent drought adaptations, whereas northern populations showed the lowest offsets, consistent with projected northward range shifts. Traits not captured by genomic offset models, such as polyploidy, clonality, hybridization, and epigenetics, may buffer environmental stress and enhance resilience, underscoring the limitations of purely genomic predictions. To complement offset estimates, we tested linear models using phenotypic data (growth, polyploidy), but the limited representation of aspen populations in common garden trials restricted validation. Nevertheless, a positive association between polyploidy and offset emerged, suggesting a potential adaptive or buffering mechanism that warrants further investigation. Together, our results illustrate both the promise and the limitations of genomic offset as a predictive framework for widely distributed species. By presenting the first approach to scale the genomic offset to a broadly-distributed forest tree, this study provides a foundation for integrating genomic, phenotypic, and ecological data to inform climate-resilient forest management. Range-wide common garden and provenance trials remain essential, yet they remain scarce for broadleaf species such as aspen. Integrating multiple data sources is critical for developing effective climate-resilient forest management strategies.

Abstract

CUE domain-containing 2 (CUEDC2) regulates ubiquitin-mediated protein turnover, cell-cycle progression, and inflammatory signaling, but its role in diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL) remains unclear. Together, these findings support a tumor cell-intrinsic CUEDC2-PP2A-B55β-NF-κB/mTOR axis in DLBCL and suggest a potentially distinct immunomodulatory role for CUEDC2. Front Immunol · 2026-10-13
Macrophage immunometabolism in osteoporosis: an emerging regulatory axis linking metabolic-polarization coupling to therapeutic targets.
Weng L, Yuan Q, Mo T, …, Ding L
▾

Abstract

Osteoporosis has long been understood, and treated, as an imbalance between osteoblastic bone formation and osteoclastic resorption. Yet many patients continue to lose bone, pointing to upstream regulators of the remodeling unit that remain poorly defined. Macrophages are developmentally continuous with osteoclasts and functionally coupled to bone-forming cells, and are now recognized as central immune regulators of skeletal homeostasis. What sets their polarization state, however, has been comparatively neglected in bone. This review adopts metabolic-polarization coupling as a unifying framework for macrophage function in osteoporosis. We argue that the metabolic program a macrophage selects acts as an upstream regulator that biases its phenotype and, in turn, the balance of bone resorption and formation, and we examine how this switch is reinforced epigenetically, amplified by comorbidity, and rendered therapeutically actionable. Aerobic glycolysis via the HIF-1α axis, versus oxidative phosphorylation and fatty-acid oxidation, sets M1 versus M2 phenotype and thereby the balance of resorption and osteogenic-vascular support. Lactate-derived post-translational modifications, notably histone lactylation, make this metabolic state durable at the epigenetic level, while metabolic comorbidities, including ageing, diabetes and obesity, amplify the axis to accelerate bone loss. We emphasize at the outset that direct intra-osseous evidence for this axis remains scarce: the skeletal phenotypes conferred by macrophage-specific deletion of HIF-1α or CPT1A are unreported, histone lactylation has yet to be quantified in bone-resident macrophages, and much of the mechanistic support stems from non-skeletal tissues, inflammatory bone-loss, or periodontal models rather than from primary postmenopausal or age-related osteoporosis. Accordingly, throughout this review we explicitly distinguish evidence established within bone from evidence merely extrapolated to bone. Repositioning macrophage metabolism from a passive correlate to an upstream causal node yields an emerging regulatory axis yields a testable sequence, metabolic node to polarization to remodeling imbalance to bone loss, that identifies metabolic enzymes such as HIF-1α and CPT1A as candidate druggable targets and polarization and lactylation signatures as candidate readouts for stratification. This perspective reframes osteoporosis therapy from suppressing mature osteoclasts toward reprogramming macrophage metabolism itself.

bioRxiv · 2026-10-11
Prion-induced heritable states in Caenorhabditis elegans.
Ilbay O, Zabinsky RA, Lozanoski T, …, Fire A
▾

Abstract

Self-replicating protein conformations can encode persistent epigenetic states that underlie prion diseases and contribute to age-related neurodegenerative disorders in mammals. In fungi, such conformers are transmitted through mitosis and meiosis, enabling robust inheritance of diverse phenotypes. Whether analogous transgenerational protein-based inheritance extends beyond unicellular systems to metazoans remains unknown. Here, we address this question in C. elegans, a genetically tractable, short-lived animal well suited for tracking the transmission of heritable information across generations. We found that a yeast prion domain, when fused to a host protein, induced a heritable altered state that arose at elevated temperatures and persisted across generations at lower temperatures. The emergence of this state depended on known prionogenic yeast repeats, while the state itself was reversible and behaved semi-dominantly in crosses, consistent with a protein-based epigenetic mechanism. These findings provide direct evidence that protein domains capable of adopting self-replicating conformers can encode heritable information in an animal.

Adv Sci (Weinh) · 2026-10-09
Lactate Signal: Modulator of Cellular Energy Production and Anabolism.
Wang H, Yang SY, Xu W
▾

Abstract

Lactate is no longer viewed simply as a glycolytic end-product, but as a compartmentalized signaling metabolite that coordinates energy production, carbon redistribution, redox balance, and anabolic commitment. This review discusses lactate as a regulator of the catabolism-anabolism axis, emphasizing two major patterns of molecular interpretation. First, lactate acts through non-covalent mechanisms, including transporter-mediated flux, receptor-dependent sensing, pH-linked effects, and direct binding to intracellular proteins. These processes allow lactate-rich states to rapidly couple metabolic flux to signaling pathways. Second, lactate-associated metabolic states are translated into the dynamic and reversible change of covalent post-translational modifications, including histone and non-histone lactylation. Histone lactylation connects glycolytic metabolism to transcriptional regulation, whereas non-histone lactylation expands lactate-dependent control to immune signaling, mitochondrial metabolism, DNA repair, cardiovascular stress, tissue remodeling, and cancer progression. We further discuss how extracellular, cytosolic, mitochondrial-associated, and nuclear lactate pools provide distinct biochemical contexts in which lactate-dependent mechanisms can operate. By integrating lactate transport, receptor sensing, protein binding, metabolism, and lactylation, this Review uses compartmental organization as a framework for synthesizing how lactate-rich states influence metabolic adaptation, stress responses, immune regulation, tissue remodeling, disease progression, and, in selected contexts, anabolic or reparative programs.

Front Immunol · 2026-10-09
Metabolic-epigenetic imprinting of macrophages in endometriosis: a working model for lesion persistence and treatment.
Wang Y, Xia W, Su X, Fang L
▾

Abstract

Endometriosis is an estrogen-dependent inflammatory disorder that often persists or returns even after visible lesions have been removed. The behavior of peritoneal macrophages may help explain this clinical durability. These cells participate in lesion implantation, vascularization, fibrosis, defective immune clearance, and pain, but it is uncertain why disease-supporting phenotypes are maintained. We argue that repeated exposure to the endometriotic niche may leave macrophages with a metabolically conditioned epigenetic memory rather than only a short-lived polarization state. Lactate, succinate, iron, oxysterols, and glutamine-derived metabolites could alter cellular metabolism while also providing substrates, cofactors, or inhibitors for chromatin-modifying enzymes. Histone lactylation and TET/JmjC-regulated methylation states are plausible molecular records of this exposure, although neither has yet been followed from metabolite to persistent mark and macrophage function in human disease. At present, the most direct endometriosis-specific support comes from a TGF-β1/CCL2-induced TET3 program associated with let-7 repression, inflammatory cytokine production, and macrophage survival. Other proposed circuits, including lactate-lactylation coupling and macrophage-stromal exchange of glycolytic signals, remain incomplete. This distinction matters: established findings support parts of the model, but not yet the entire causal chain. A memory state should be experimentally identifiable by its persistence after stimulus withdrawal, transferability, metabolic dependence, and reversibility. If experiments satisfy these criteria, the retained macrophage state would become a plausible non-hormonal target. The relevant test would be whether changing that state produces a lasting loss of lesion-supporting function instead of a brief reduction in downstream inflammation.

Zhou K, Song X, Jiang Y, …, Chen M

Abstract

Growing evidence suggests that exposure to metals, such as molybdenum, contributes to significant health risks. Human studies revealed that Mo exposure was associated with an increased risk of male infertility via 17α-OHP. This study innovatively elucidated the mechanisms by which paternal Mo exposure induced transgenerational male reproductive toxicity through metabolic and epigenetic alterations using human, mouse, and in vitro models.

Yang H, Li Y, Meng H, …, Liu F
▾

Abstract

BACKGROUND: While photochemical damage mechanisms have been extensively characterized, thermal damage from infrared (IR) radiation and environmental heat persists as an underappreciated pathophysiological entity. Current photoprotection standards focus exclusively on ultraviolet (UV) blocking, leaving skin vulnerable to thermally-induced aging and systemic deterioration. OBJECTIVE: To systematically delineate skin thermal damage as distinct from photoaging through comparative molecular energetics analysis, propose an operational scientific definition, and elucidate the paradigm shift from "photon blocking" to "systemic conditioning" for effective thermal protection. METHODS: Comprehensive literature synthesis (2022-2025) focusing on epigenetic aging studies, TRPV1 thermosensory mechanisms, heat shock protein dynamics, and multi-targeted pharmacological interventions. RESULTS: Thermal damage operates through physical energy accumulation (molecular vibrational excitation) rather than photochemical bond cleavage, with TRPV1/Ca2+/HSF1 signaling constituting a pathway independent of UV-induced DNA damage. Recent evidence demonstrates that chronic heat exposure accelerates epigenetic aging by 14 months-equivalent to smoking effects (Choi 2025)-through DNAmAge acceleration. Notably, IR effects exhibit dose-dependent biphasicity: low-dose photobiomodulation (<10 J/cm2) confers beneficial preconditioning via mild HSP induction, whereas high-dose thermal accumulation (>42°C tissue temperature) triggers destructive protein denaturation and MMP-1 upregulation. CONCLUSIONS: Skin thermal damage represents a "physical dissolution" process characterized by cumulative internal structural disruption, necessitating protection strategies fundamentally different from UV blocking. Traditional Chinese Medicine offers unique advantages through multi-targeted "conditioning" of cellular resilience mechanisms, aligning with the holistic requirements of thermal protection.

Abstract

Abstract

Multicellular self-organization depends on interactions across multiple length scales, yet mapping protein states at high spatial resolution in whole-mount embryos remains challenging. Here, we introduce high-throughput 3D in toto iterative immunofluorescence imaging (3D-4i) and a dedicated computer vision pipeline to quantify morphological and molecular features from subcellular to whole-embryo scales across hundreds of samples. Applying this pipeline to early zebrafish embryos undergoing mid-blastula transition, we determine the cell cycle phase for each cell across the embryo, and uncover the spatiotemporal dynamics by which global meta-synchronous mitotic waves transition to cell cycle desynchronization. Using statistical analysis, we find that the cell cycle phase is the major source of variability in transcription within a division cycle, and combining this with the analysis of key transcription factors and chromatin modifier state, included in our multiplexed dataset, enables accurate prediction of transcriptional output during zygotic genome activation in individual cells. Together, these findings establish 3D-4i as a powerful approach for quantifying multimodal, multiscale biological processes underlying multicellular self-organization.

Nat Commun · IF 14.7 · 2026-10-12
Bacterial inoculation elicits changes to the coral epigenome.
Barno AR, Villela HDM, Cardoso PM, …, Peixoto RS
▾

Abstract

Environmental shifts can cause epigenetic modifications in corals associated with changes in gene expression and physiology, although it remains unclear if associated bacteria can also induce such changes. Here, we inoculate nubbins of the coral Pocillopora verrucosa with an opportunistic pathogen, Vibrio coralliilyticus, and/or a coral probiotic, Cobetia sp., and subject the nubbins to heat stress. We show that pathogen exposure leads to distinct DNA methylation changes compared to the control, probiotic, and co-inoculation groups. Although many aspects remain to be resolved, evidence that bacteria alter coral epigenomes may help clarify the role of microbiome interactions in coral resilience.

Schulz M, Shao X, Lambrot R, …, Kimmins S
▾

Abstract

The occurrence of childhood neurodevelopmental disorders has been steadily increasing for decades, yet we have little understanding of modes of inheritance implicated in these diseases. Epidemiology studies have revealed an association between an elevated paternal Body Mass Index (BMI) and age as factors implicated in increased risk for neurodevelopmental disorders in children. Epigenetic regulation of gene expression by DNA methylation at cytosines is susceptible to environmental, diet, and lifestyle changes. Yet, whether a man's BMI and age influence the sperm methylome and potentially impact offspring development remains unresolved. EMBO Rep · IF 6.5 · 2026-11-03
Self-organization of Drosophila chromatin architecture in a cell-free system.
Jayakrishnan M, Kars G, Campos-Sparr A, …, Becker PB
▾

Abstract

Metazoan genomes are organized by folding of the nucleosome fiber into loops and domains that support long-range regulatory interactions. Although cohesin-mediated loop extrusion and architectural DNA-binding proteins are central to current models of genome organization, how these mechanisms integrate to generate higher-order structure remains incompletely understood. Early Drosophila melanogaster embryogenesis provides a unique window into the emergence of chromatin architecture, as rapid syncytial nuclear divisions occur largely in the absence of transcription. However, probing the mechanisms underlying this primordial folding in vivo is technically challenging. Here, we establish an in vitro system that reconstitutes complex chromatin using extracts from syncytial embryos. Nucleosome mapping and Micro-C analyses reveal that long-range interactions, including cis and trans loops and topologically associating domains (TADs), emerge spontaneously from soluble extract components. While some structures resemble those observed in early embryos, others represent latent interaction potentials that are constrained in vivo. Focusing on the eve locus, we find that TAD formation is incompatible with a simple loop extrusion model and instead requires direct pairing of boundary elements mediated by the insulator protein 'Suppressor-of-hairy-wing' Su(Hw). Together, our work demonstrates that key features of 3D genome organization can be reconstituted in a cell-free system and provides a tractable platform for the mechanistic dissection of chromatin folding in Drosophila.

Genome Res · IF 6.2 · 2026-10-13
Parental genome reprogramming drives allelic convergence while preserving parent-of-origin memory during human early embryonic development.
Xu F, Liu Q, Du Z, …, Yan L
▾

Abstract

Human sperm and oocytes carry highly distinct DNA methylation landscapes, yet parental DNA methylation ultimately converges to a largely equivalent state, raising the question of when and how the allelic DNA methylome is reprogrammed toward convergence. Here, we present an integrated allelic analysis of DNA methylation and transcriptome dynamics throughout human pre- to peri-implantation development, a stage that lays the foundation for future development. We find that oocyte-specific hypermethylated regions retain maternal-biased DNA methylation asymmetry throughout early development, whereas paternal hypermethylation at sperm-specific regions is erased after fertilization and re-established during peri-implantation. Whole-genome parental DNA methylation levels converge across all lineages at embryonic days 8 to 12, prior to gastrulation, although the timing of this equilibrium differs among genomic elements. Notably, a subset of transposable elements, including SVAs and LTR12 elements, remains persistently maternally hypermethylated. Finally, we identify parent-of-origin-biased genes involved in early human embryonic development and find that these genes are enriched for dosage-sensitive genes. We further show that epiblast-specific parental expression bias is associated with genes implicated in neurodevelopmental disorders. These findings highlight the developmental and clinical relevance of allele-specific DNA methylation and transcription regulation during the earliest stages of human development.

Hum Reprod · IF 6.1 · 2026-10-30
Epididymis-derived RNAs in sperm affect the next generation: potential clinical implications.
Chen AJ, Meyers AJ, Rainsford S, …, Sakkas D
▾

Abstract

Until recently, the sole function of sperm was thought to be as delivery agents for the paternal contribution of zygote genes encoded in the DNA they carry. However, recent research has shown that sperm contribute substantially more information to oocytes upon fertilization through a select population of non-coding (nc)RNAs obtained while traversing the epididymis. Strikingly, their content can be altered by the environment in which males have lived, such as their exposure to various types of pollutants, traumas, addictive drugs, diet, and other factors. These changes in sperm ncRNA content can be responsible for transmitting the negative consequences of these environments to their offspring, via 'epigenetic inheritance', which is reminiscent of Lamarckian inheritance. This mini-review aims to provide an overview of the role of environment-regulated sperm ncRNAs in this process. We include studies on the relationship between specific paternal environments and the associated changes in sperm ncRNA content. These studies have been performed largely in rodent models, but we also describe evidence supporting the same processes occurring in humans. As an example, we focus on the epigenetic inheritance of stress phenotypes through changes in sperm miRNA profiles. We propose that identifying these specific ncRNAs carried by human sperm could lead to future diagnostic and treatment paradigms positively impacting ART. Screening out these potentially harmful sperm, or further research that provides approaches to reverse these changes before conception, may therefore prevent negative consequences related to epigenetic inheritance.

Development · IF 3.7 · 2026-10-26
Test of the DNMT1o imprinted maintenance methylation model reveals progressive methylation loss between 8-cell and blastocyst stages.
Matoushaya CN, Pena-Bello CA, Shaffer B, …, Mann MRW
▾

Abstract

DNMT1o, the maternal oocyte-derived isoform of DNA methyltransferase 1, is a key protein to maintain imprinted methylation. A model was proposed in 2001, and refined in 2008, where DNMT1o acts to maintain imprinted methylation exclusively at the 8-cell stage during preimplantation development. Nearly 20 years later, this is the first comprehensive analysis testing the maternal DNMT1o imprinted maintenance methylation model. We analyzed Snrpn and H19 imprinted methylation in individual control and maternal Dnmt1o-deficient mouse embryos across five preimplantation stages. Our findings challenged assumptions that DNMT1o acted solely at the 8-cell stage to produce ∼50% methylation loss in all embryos. Compared to controls, maternal Dnmt1o-deficient embryos experienced (1) progressive Snrpn and H19 imprinted methylation loss between the 8-cell to blastocyst stages, with inter-embryo variability extending beyond the predicted 50% loss; cleavage stage embryos exhibited similar methylation levels; (2) some embryos maintained maternal Snrpn and paternal H19 methylation, bypassing imprinted methylation loss; and (3) at the DNA strand level, Snrpn and H19 methylation loss was sporadic rather than complete. These findings point to potential compensation mechanisms during early preimplantation development.

Decoding chromatin-associated RNA (caRNA) interaction networks: from genome-wide dynamic capture to epigenetic mechanisms in plants and animals.
Liu C, Li W, Lv Q, Su R
▾

Abstract

Chromatin-associated RNAs (caRNAs) serve as central hubs for regulation within the cell nucleus and are involved in key biological processes such as transcriptional homeostasis, chromatin remodeling, and transgenerational inheritance. In recent years, the development of various genome-wide RNA-chromatin interaction capture technologies has made it possible to resolve the spatial interaction networks between caRNAs and chromatin with high precision. Curr Pharm Des · 2026-11-03
Hesperidin Enhances Doxorubicin Cytotoxicity in MCF-7 Breast Cancer Cells: Evidence from Cellular, Gene Expression, Global DNA Methylation, and Molecular Docking Analyses.
Abdel Moneim RM, El-Baz HA, Elshal MF
▾

Abstract

INTRODUCTION/OBJECTIVE: Doxorubicin efficacy in breast cancer is constrained by chemoresistance and toxicity. Hesperidin, a citrus flavonoid, exhibits anticancer and epigenetic regulatory properties. This study evaluated whether hesperidin enhances doxorubicin cytotoxicity in MCF-7 cells and characterized the associated apoptotic and Heat-Shock (HSP60, HSP90) transcripts, as well as global DNA methylation. METHODS: Cytotoxicity and synergy were assessed using the MTT assay and the Chou-Talalay method (CI). Apoptosis and cell cycle were analyzed by flow cytometry. Expression of BAX, BCL2, HSP60, and HSP90 was quantified using RT-qPCR. Global DNA methylation (5-mC%) was assessed colorimetrically, alongside molecular docking simulations for HSP60 and HSP90. RESULTS: Combining hesperidin with doxorubicin produced synergistic cytotoxicity (CI < 1) and a dosereduction index of 1.95 for doxorubicin. The combination induced apoptosis, accompanied by upregulation of BAX, downregulation of BCL2, and modulation of HSP60/HSP90 transcript levels. Additionally, the combination attenuated doxorubicin-induced global hypermethylation. Molecular docking demonstrated favorable binding affinities of hesperidin toward both HSP60 and HSP90, particularly HSP60; however, these findings remain correlative without proving direct target engagement. DISCUSSION: The synergistic response, paired with modulated apoptotic markers and global DNA methylation, suggests that hesperidin sensitizes MCF-7 cells to doxorubicin through multi-targeted cellular and epigenetic pathways. While docking and transcriptomic shifts indicate potential chaperone involvement, these preliminary observations require further functional validation. CONCLUSION: Hesperidin enhances doxorubicin cytotoxicity in MCF-7 cells by modulating apoptosis, stressresponse transcripts, and DNA methylation. These in-vitro findings support further mechanistic and in-vivo evaluation of this combination strategy.

Front Immunol · 2026-11-03
Understanding and translating evolutionary adaptations in animals exposed to contaminated diets to guide strategies against human foodborne disease: a prioritisation framework.
Abaidullah M, Ji Y, Huang Q, …, Xu D
▾

Abstract

BACKGROUND: Humans face increasing vulnerability to foodborne and infectious diseases despite medical advances, while certain animal lineages cope with pathogen-laden diets and environments under the particular ecological pressures that selected their defences. Animals are not universally resistant, and wildlife and livestock outbreaks of Salmonella, Campylobacter, Vibrio and mycotoxins are documented; but certain lineages have evolved tolerance or rapid-clearance mechanisms with minimal pathology. This disparity arises from evolutionary adaptations in animals, contrasting with human reliance on hygiene and antibiotics, which contribute to antimicrobial resistance and zoonotic spillovers, while modern sanitation has also delivered dramatic reductions in waterborne disease mortality. AIM OF REVIEW: This review aims to synthesize evidence on animal disease resistance mechanisms and translate these insights into clinical strategies for improving human health, addressing gaps in comparative biology and combating emerging threats like antimicrobial resistance. We introduce the Biomimetic Immunity Continuum, a translational framework for comparative immunology built on an explicit selection rule that rejects candidate adaptations as well as ranking them; four are rejected here. It maps animal defence pillars (genetic, ecological, behavioural, physiological, microbiome) along a spectrum from prevention to molecular tuning to therapeutic replacement, prioritizing feasibility, reversibility, safety, and scalability. These criteria are applied as three exclusion gates and four ranked criteria, with candidates ordered on the limiting step rather than on a composite numerical score. We predict that, among adaptations acting on conserved or reachable human pathways, translational potential depends more on conservation, accessibility, safety and deliverability than on the size of the phenotype in the source animal. Throughout, we distinguish avoidance (reduction of exposure), resistance (reduction of pathogen or toxin burden), tolerance (reduction of health cost per unit burden) and resilience (the composite property emerging when these operate together), because these axes carry different translational and safety implications. KEY SCIENTIFIC CONCEPTS OF REVIEW: Animals employ multifaceted defences: genetic (e.g., immune gene evolution), ecological (e.g., habitat avoidance), behavioural (e.g., grooming), physiological (e.g., vultures' hyperacidic stomachs neutralizing anthrax), immunological (e.g., epigenetic reprogramming), and microbial (e.g., microbiome-mediated exclusion). These synergize to prevent infections, as seen in bats tolerating high viral loads with minimal immunopathology. Candidate human applications differ widely in maturity: trained immunity via BCG and defined live biotherapeutic products are already in human use, dietary interventions mimicking animal microbiomes have reached controlled trials, and heat-shock induction has human data but no controlled trial against a foodborne endpoint. We separate mechanistic inspiration from candidate intervention throughout, and the evidence level attached to each proposal says which it is. Bridging evolutionary and clinical science offers candidate approaches to disease prevention. Every proposal is classified by translational maturity on a five-level scale from approved human use to source-species observation alone; this records maturity rather than certainty or demonstrated efficacy. Animal exemplars are included only where they meet stated relevance criteria for foodborne hazards as defined by the World Health Organization, which treats infectious and chemical hazards together.

▾

Abstract

Parental TCPP exposure impaired reproduction and offspring developmental in zebrafish; yet its multigenerational and transgenerational effects and epigenetic mechanisms in estuarine fish remain unknown. Noise Health · 2026-11-03
Research Progress on the Mechanisms Underlying the Effects of Prenatal Noise Exposure on Fetal Neurodevelopment and Birth Outcomes: A Review.
Chen J, Zhou C
▾

Abstract

With the intensification of urbanization, noise pollution has emerged as a major public health concern. It is critical to distinguish between environmental noise (e.g., traffic and community noise) and occupational noise (e.g., industrial machinery), as their exposure patterns, regulatory frameworks, and intervention strategies differ substantially. Given that pregnancy constitutes a critical sensitive window for early-life development, the potential impact of prenatal noise exposure on fetal neurodevelopment and birth outcomes has attracted considerable attention. In this article, we systematically review the research progress in this field. In epidemiological studies, conclusions regarding the association between noise exposure and adverse birth outcomes remain inconclusive. Furthermore, longitudinal human cohort studies investigating the relationship between noise exposure and offspring neurodevelopment are scarce, with the relevant evidence being largely derived from extrapolation from animal experiments. Animal model studies have confirmed that prenatal noise exposure induces cognitive and behavioral abnormalities in offspring, alongside underlying impairments, including disrupted synaptic plasticity and suppressed neurogenesis. Notably, these effects exhibit pronounced sex specificity. Interventions, including melatonin administration and exposure to an enriched environment, have been shown to effectively alleviate the resulting adverse effects. At the molecular level, prenatal noise exposure primarily shapes fetal neurodevelopmental programming through three core pathways: aberrant activation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, induction of oxidative stress and neuroinflammation, and dysregulated epigenetic modifications. Current research still faces several key methodological challenges, including the lack of standardized exposure assessment methods, inadequate control for confounding factors, and ambiguous definitions that delineate the boundary between beneficial acoustic stimulation and detrimental noise exposure. Future studies should prioritize conducting large-scale prospective cohort studies, developing standardized animal models, exploring transgenerational epigenetic effects, and devising targeted intervention strategies, thereby establishing a robust scientific foundation for prenatal noise protection and clinical fetal health management.

▾

Abstract

Paternal exposure to ambient fine particulate matter (PM2.5) may affect metabolic health in progeny, while its multigenerational phenotypes and epigenetic underpinnings remain incompletely understood. Male C57BL/6J mice received filtered air (FA), unfiltered ambient air (UA), or concentrated ambient PM2.5 (CAP) inhalation exposure (6h/day, 5 days/week, 22 weeks). F1 offspring were derived from matings between exposed F0 males and unexposed females, and F2 and F3 were sequentially generated by crossing unexposed male descendants from the preceding generation with unexposed females. F0 sperm harbored abundant DMRs enriched in lipid metabolism and liver toxicity pathways. Apoe and Apoa2 presented concordant hypermethylation changes in F0 sperm and offspring liver. Gao Q, Yan JM, Zhang FL, …, Li L
▾

Abstract

During mammalian oocyte maturation, meiotic arrest takes place during prophase I at the germinal vesicle (GV) stage. Under a specific hormonal milieu, oocytes are reactivated to complete meiosis I and move to a stage of maturity at metaphase II during meiosis II. The regulatory mechanisms underlying this arrest remain incompletely understood. In summary, our findings highlight that ZBTB33 potentially regulates bovine oocyte meiosis by orchestrating chromatin accessibility at CCNB1 and PAPOLG. These new findings provide novel insight into the epigenetic regulation of meiotic progression in bovine oocytes.

J Chem Ecol · 2026-10-31
Sulforaphane, A Mustard Oil Bomb Product, Reprograms Herbivore Gene Expression and Alters Chromatin Structure Across Generations.
Arnold TM, Somers DJ, Kushner DB, …, Arnold M
▾

Abstract

Sulforaphane (SFN), an isothiocyanate produced by the mustard oil bomb defense system of cruciferous plants, is a natural inhibitor of class I and II histone deacetylases (HDACs). We previously showed that dietary SFN inhibits HDAC activity, delays larval development, and alters the expression of more than 1,600 genes in the agricultural pests Spodoptera exigua and Trichoplusia ni. Here we investigated the mechanisms underlying these responses and whether their effects extend across generations. RNA-seq analyses revealed that SFN rapidly disrupted the normal developmental progression of gene expression in both somatic and germline tissues. ATAC-seq analyses indicated that these transcriptional changes in S. exigua were accompanied by widespread alterations in chromatin accessibility. In this species, we detected the suppression of genes involved in energy metabolism, consistent with the observed developmental delays. We observed that many SFN-induced changes in chromatin accessibility and gene expression persisted in the offspring of exposed insects despite the offspring never consuming SFN themselves. These findings demonstrate that SFN acts through chromatin-associated mechanisms to alter transcriptional programs during development, with intergenerational effects. We propose that SFN, and perhaps other HDAC inhibitors produced by plants in nature, may act as an "epigenetic weapon" with the potential to influence herbivore gene expression across generations.

Regulatory roles and mechanistic insights of chromatin 3D structure in mammalian growth and development: A research progress review.
Zhao N, Sun Y, Tang X, …, Li N
▾

Abstract

Three-dimensional (3D) genome organization is closely linked to gene regulation and cell fate during mammalian development. This review examines how 3D chromatin architecture is established and dynamically remodeled from gametogenesis and early embryogenesis to postnatal development and disease. Rather than providing a conventional summary of hierarchical genome organization, we focus on the interplay among loop extrusion, biomolecular condensation, transcriptional and RNA-mediated regulation, and developmental context. Particular attention is given to species-associated differences in early embryonic genome reconstruction and to the unresolved relationship between chromatin architecture, transcriptional regulation, and developmental outcomes. We also discuss emerging technologies and their limitations in distinguishing structural associations from causal regulatory relationships.

Pharmacological profiling reveals sensitivity of vitamin D/TGF-β cooperative gene regulation to epigenetic and transcription-associated inhibitors.
Mathes M, Oberlis JH, Häfner AK, …, Steinhilber D
▾

Abstract

Synergistic gene regulation by the active vitamin D metabolite, 1,25(OH)2D3 and transforming growth factor β (TGF-β) has been reported for a limited number of target genes. Among these, 5-lipoxygenase (5-LO), a key enzyme in leukotriene biosynthesis and innate immune signaling, represents a well-established example of this cooperative regulation. However, the molecular mechanisms underlying this synergy between vitamin D and TGF-β remain incompletely understood. RNA-Sequencing identified additional genes synergistically regulated by combined 1,25(OH)2D3/TGF-β stimulation that were enriched for immune-related functions and displayed similar sensitivity to pharmacological disruption of transcriptional and chromatin regulatory mechanisms. Furthermore, VDR knockout experiments demonstrated that intact VDR signaling is required for 1,25(OH)2D3/TGF-β mediated cooperative gene regulation. These findings reveal a broader set of immune-associated genes regulated by 1,25(OH)2D3/TGF-β cooperation and identify chromatin and transcriptional regulators as important modulators of this response in monocytic cells.

Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr · 2026-10-29
Fructose as a systemic metabolic signal orchestrating metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease pathogenesis.
Li C, Li M, Wang B, Ji G
▾

Abstract

Metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD) affects approximately 30% of the global population, yet the specific role of dietary fructose in disease progression remains inadequately defined. This review delineates the "Fructose-ectopic lipid accumulation (ELA)-MASLD" axis, identifying refined fructose (e.g., from sugar-sweetened beverages) not merely as a substrate but as a systemic signal that coordinates a pathological metabolic network. Hepatic fructose metabolism via the ketohexokinase-C (KHK-C) pathway bypasses the rate-limiting phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1) checkpoint, promotes unregulated de novo lipogenesis (DNL), ATP depletion, and organellar stress. We identify the functional decline of subcutaneous adipose tissue (SAT) plasticity as an important driver of this lipotoxic cascade. This tends to result in systemic lipid spillover, promoting an inflammatory cascade that impairs the intestinal barrier and induces insulin resistance (IR) in skeletal muscle and bystander organs. Furthermore, we discuss how genetic variants amplify this toxicity and critically evaluate emerging preclinical hypotheses regarding epigenetic remodeling and transgenerational inheritance. Finally, we propose stratified management strategies that integrate evidence-based nutritional modifications with targeted pharmacotherapy, distinguishing between hepatic-dominant and adipose-derived lipid excess to optimize clinical outcomes.

Toxicol Lett · 2026-10-29
Effects of perfluorohexane sulfonic acid on female reproductive function and oocyte quality.
Xu M, Ma Q, Jin S, …, Lu Y
▾

Abstract

An emerging persistent organic pollutant, perfluorohexane sulfonic acid (PFHxS), has drawn considerable attention owing to its female reproductive toxicity. PFHxS penetrates the blood-follicle barrier and accumulates in follicular fluid, with a prolonged elimination half-life in humans. Mechanistically, PFHxS impairs oocyte quality through oxidative stress, mitochondrial damage, disruption of hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis signaling, meiotic spindle abnormalities, and epigenetic modifications. Animal models demonstrate that PFHxS exposure leads to follicular arrest, reduced ovulation, and intrauterine growth restriction in offspring. Human studies have associated exposure with diminished ovarian reserve, prolonged amenorrhea, and reduced high-quality embryos. Current research highlights a shift from single-compound to mixture effects and transgenerational epigenetic inheritance, yet significant gaps remain regarding chronic low-dose exposure and causal evidence in clinical populations. Future research should focus on realistic exposure models and large-scale prospective cohorts, and the existing evidence should be integrated into regulatory frameworks to safeguard reproductive health.

Biomolecular condensates orchestrate small RNA pathways and transgenerational inheritance.
Chen L, Wang K
▾

Abstract

Ribonucleoprotein (RNP) granules are biomolecular condensates composed of RNAs and RNA-binding proteins that help organize gene expression, small RNA pathways, and genome surveillance. Stachovicova S, Benc M, Strejcek F, …, Laurincik J
▾

Abstract

Oocyte maturity significantly influences developmental competence. Although individual aspects of oocyte quality have been relatively well characterized and supported by various methodological approaches, their interconnection and comprehensive evaluation are often lacking. On the one hand, cytoplasmic maturation plays an important role. On the other hand, chromatin configurational changes lead to the progressive silencing of transcriptional activity. Considerably less attention, however, has been devoted to the unique structure known as the nucleolus-like body (NLB), which plays a key role in chromatin organization and may significantly influence its remodelling during oocyte maturation. Together, these findings demonstrate that the proposed classification identifies oocyte populations differing in chromatin organization and developmental competence and provide further evidence of an association between NLB properties and the acquisition of developmental competence.

Int J Mol Sci · 2026-10-27
Molecular Genetics and Reproductive Genomic Medicine in Cleft Lip and Palate: Current Evidence, Preimplantation Testing, and Clinical Translation-A Narrative Review.
Fan R, Shen H, Liu Y, …, Ma X
▾

Abstract

Cleft lip and palate (CLP) is a common congenital craniofacial anomaly with heterogeneous clinical manifestations and complex etiologies. Current evidence indicates that CLP arises from the combined effects of Mendelian syndromic disorders, rare pathogenic variants, common susceptibility loci, non-coding regulatory variants, epigenetic regulation, mitochondrial dysfunction, and maternal environmental exposures. Although reconstructive surgery and multidisciplinary care remain central to treatment, families with recurrent CLP or a family history of orofacial clefts increasingly seek reproductive counseling and genetic risk assessment before pregnancy. Preimplantation genetic testing (PGT) may provide a clinically meaningful option when a pathogenic variant, chromosomal abnormality, or clearly defined familial haplotype is identified. However, most non-syndromic CLP cases are polygenic or multifactorial, and the application of preimplantation genetic testing for polygenic disorders (PGT-P) remains limited by modest predictive accuracy, population-specific transferability, embryo-number constraints, incomplete penetrance, variants of uncertain significance, and uncertain long-term clinical utility. This narrative review summarizes the clinical phenotypes and genomic architecture of CLP, evaluates the principles and current limitations of preimplantation genetic testing for monogenic disorders (PGT-M) and PGT-P, and discusses psychosocial and ethical issues in reproductive decision-making. We propose that PGT for CLP should be positioned as part of a stepwise counseling framework rather than as a universal preventive strategy. Future progress will depend on standardized phenotyping, multi-ancestry genomic databases, functional validation of candidate variants, transparent risk communication, and shared decision-making that respects both reproductive autonomy and the uncertainty of genomic prediction.

Int J Mol Sci · 2026-10-27
Endometriosis-Associated Infertility: Molecular Mechanisms and Evidence-Based Clinical Management.
Chang KJ, Lai TH
▾

Abstract

Endometriosis is a chronic, estrogen-dependent inflammatory disease and a major contributor to female infertility. Endometriosis-associated infertility arises from interacting anatomical and biological mechanisms, including pelvic adhesions and tubal dysfunction, reduced ovarian reserve, altered follicular and oocyte competence, oxidative stress, immune dysregulation, and impaired endometrial function. This narrative review integrates current concepts of disease pathogenesis with evidence-based fertility management. It first examines the molecular, cellular, and immune processes that support ectopic lesion establishment and may impair reproduction. It then discusses diagnostic phenotyping, infertility evaluation, prognostic tools, medical suppression, fertility-preserving surgery, intrauterine insemination, and in vitro fertilization. Particular attention is given to the strength and clinical relevance of current evidence, including surgery for ovarian endometrioma before assisted reproduction, prolonged gonadotropin-releasing hormone agonist pretreatment, fresh versus frozen embryo transfer, and the use of intracytoplasmic sperm injection without male-factor infertility. Emerging anti-angiogenic, immunomodulatory, antioxidant, stem-cell, and gene- or epigenetic-based interventions remain investigational. Because neither a single treatment nor a single assisted-reproduction protocol is optimal for all patients, management should integrate age, ovarian reserve, symptom burden, disease phenotype, prior surgery, coexisting infertility factors, and reproductive priorities. Mechanistically informed clinical trials using live birth and patient-centered outcomes are needed to advance precision fertility care.

▾

Abstract

Rice faces increasing threats from climate change, driven by heat, salinity, and drought stresses. This review comprehensively synthesizes current knowledge of the integrated signaling networks and adaptive mechanisms underlying rice responses to heat, salinity, and drought stress, with particular emphasis on stress crosstalk and multi-stress responses. By integrating current knowledge across molecular, physiological, and breeding perspectives, we provide a comprehensive integrated signaling network model and crosstalk among signaling pathways for understanding rice adaptation to combined stresses and highlight potential strategies for enhancing crop resilience and global food security.

HERV transcription and its silencing machinery are stably maintained in granulosa cells across oocyte maturation stages, suggesting a tightly regulated transcriptional equilibrium within the follicular microenvironment.

Acute heat stress induces short- and long-term epigenetic changes in the buff-tailed bumblebee (Bombus terrestris).
Renard T, Boseret M, Aron S
▾

Abstract

BACKGROUND: Global warming and the increasing frequency of extreme weather events threaten organisms worldwide, with cold-adapted species like bumblebees being especially vulnerable. Although heat stress has been shown to affect bumblebee physiology and behaviour, whether it also impacts their epigenome remains unknown. In this study, we addressed this gap by investigating the short- and long-term effects of acute heat stress experienced early in adult life on DNA methylation profiles in the buff-tailed bumblebee (Bombus terrestris). The DNA methylomes of males exposed either to control conditions (25°C) or to acute heat stress (42 °C for 60 min) were sequenced 1 day and 15 days after exposure to disentangle the short- and long-term effects of thermal stress. RESULTS: Heat stress induced differential methylation at hundreds to thousands of sites in both the short- and long-term, yet these signatures were largely distinct across time points. Accordingly, random forest classification failed to identify a persistent long-term DNA methylation signature of heat stress, indicating that heat-induced DNA methylation patterns change over time. Similarly, epigenetic clock analyses revealed that epigenetic age was transiently increased in the short-term, but not in the long-term. CONCLUSIONS: By uncovering the temporal dynamics of DNA methylation profiles following heat stress inB. terrestrismales, our findings suggest that DNA methylation is actively regulated rather than passively maintaining environmentally induced alterations. These results shed new light on how environmental signals may dynamically shape the insect epigenome.

Abstract

BACKGROUND: Male infertility accounts for nearly half of all infertility cases, worldwide, and is frequently associated with molecular abnormalities that cannot be detected by routine semen analysis. Actin-like protein 7A (ACTL7A), a testis-specific actin-related protein essential for spermatogenesis, acrosome integrity, and oocyte activation, has recently emerged as a potential regulator of sperm function. This study aimed to investigate the association between ACTL7A expression and sperm DNA integrity in infertile men. Consistently, individuals with high sperm DNA damage displayed a pronounced reduction in ACTL7A expression, with RNA levels decreased by ~3.4-fold and protein levels by ~2.2-fold, compared to those with low DNA damage. CONCLUSION: ACTL7A may serve as a potential indicator of sperm DNA integrity and motility, warranting further validation as a biomarker.

Wang P, Tang W, Mou Y, …, Huang X
▾

Abstract

Articular cartilage homeostasis and osteoarthritis (OA) progression are strongly influenced by hydrostatic pressure (HP) within the joint mechanical microenvironment. Physiological-range HP (approximately 0.1-10 MPa in most experimental models) can maintain the chondrocyte phenotype, promote chondrogenic differentiation of stem/progenitor cells, support extracellular matrix (ECM) anabolism, and attenuate inflammatory, oxidative, and apoptotic signaling. By contrast, sustained or high-magnitude HP, particularly ≥20 MPa in injurious in vitro models, commonly shifts cartilage cells toward catabolism and cellular stress. Mechanistically, HP engages wingless-related integration site/β-catenin (Wnt/β-catenin), mitogen-activated protein kinase/extracellular signal-regulated kinase (MAPK/ERK), Hippo-Yes-associated protein (Hippo-YAP), noncoding RNA, epigenetic, heat-shock protein, and transient receptor potential (TRP) channel networks. Distinct from earlier reviews focused primarily on chondrogenesis or broad cartilage mechanobiology, this review integrates healthy and OA chondrocytes with progenitor-cell responses, compares HP delivery and culture systems, reconciles apparently contradictory parameter-response data, and evaluates translational opportunities and limitations. These comparisons support evidence-informed starting ranges for future HP protocols, while emphasizing that no single regimen is universally optimal.

Toxics · 2026-10-26
Research Progress on Genotoxicity and Molecular Mechanisms of Environmental Endocrine Disruptors.
Xia J, Li J, Yang Y, …, Chen T
▾

Abstract

Genotoxicity can be transmitted across generations through germ cells, thereby substantially increasing long-term health risks. Environmental endocrine disruptors (EEDs) are ubiquitously present, resistant to degradation, and prone to biomagnification along food chains, ultimately leading to human exposure and multi-level genetic damage (from chromosomal aberrations to DNA strand breaks and base modifications). At first, this review outlines the five major sources of EEDs and summarizes current genotoxicological detection approaches, including the Ames test, comet assay, micronucleus test, γH2AX-DDR co-detection, error-corrected sequencing, and multi-omics technologies. Subsequently, based on classical animal and cellular models, we systematically evaluate the research progress and mechanistic insights into the genotoxicity of both traditional and emerging EEDs over the past fifteen years. Our review reveals that bisphenols, phthalates, polybrominated biphenyls (PBDEs), and some novel contaminants (e.g., BPA substitutes and PFAS alternatives) consistently induce DNA damage, chromosomal abnormalities, and micronucleus formation. The underlying core mechanisms converge on genotoxic insult driven by reactive oxygen species (ROS)-mediated oxidative stress, interference with DNA damage response (DDR) pathways, and epigenetic dysregulation. Notably, some novel BPA substitutes exhibit even greater toxicity than their parent compounds, and combined exposures, even at environmentally relevant low doses, can provoke significant genetic lesions and pose transgenerational risks. In light of current knowledge gaps, we propose that future research should leverage high-throughput screening, integrative multi-omics, and artificial intelligence to focus on low-dose, long-term, and co-exposure scenarios, thereby providing robust scientific evidence for genotoxicity risk assessment. This review not only supplies foundational data for evaluating the genotoxic hazards and health risks of EEDs to humans, but also delineates a forward-looking roadmap for environmental genetic toxicology research.

These findings suggest that early-life thermal stress may result in epigenetic alterations linked to physiological traits, highlighting DNA methylation as a potential mechanism underlying developmental plasticity to climate-related stressors in fish.

Abstract

Meiotic chromosome segregation requires the earlier coordination of chromosome morphogenesis with cell-cycle progression. Transgenerationally inherited incisor aberration mediated by stress in two isolated populations of Microtus hartingi vole.
Kaija L, Zorenko TA
▾

Abstract

Environmental and social stress (SS) can alter phenotypic expression at both individual and population levels. We examined the effects of ancestral SS, imposed through forced polygyny, on reproductive success and maxillary incisor overgrowth in 2 isolated populations of Microtus hartingi vole: from Central Anatolia (CAP) and Eastern Rhodope (RP). In addition to ancestral SS, CAP voles were exposed to environmental stress during prenatal, lactation, and early-life periods. CAP, originating from more stable environments, can be considered as a model of the early stage of RP adaptation to habitat fragmentation, as their heightened stress responsiveness leads to pronounced fluctuations in incisor overgrowth levels. Together, these findings suggest that stress-induced regulatory effects can persist across generations and may contribute to population divergence.

Maternal mental health and its intergenerational transmission in-utero: a scoping review.
Seema M, Afoakwah C, Byrnes J
▾

Abstract

Maternal mental health during pregnancy is important for maternal wellbeing and offspring development. Prenatal depression, anxiety, stress, and psychological distress have been associated with offspring emotional, behavioural, cognitive, and mental health outcomes, but evidence remains dispersed across conditions, outcomes, and proposed mechanisms. This scoping review synthesises empirical evidence on maternal mental health during pregnancy as a potential intergenerational exposure, including potential in-utero pathways.Five databases (MEDLINE, Scopus, PsycInfo, CINAHL, and Embase) were searched from 1995 to 24 February 2026, following PRISMA-ScR guidance. Eligible sources of evidence examined maternal mental health during pregnancy in relation to postnatal offspring outcomes. Data were extracted using a structured framework and synthesised narratively. Twenty sources of evidence met the inclusion criteria. Across diverse designs and populations, prenatal depression, anxiety, stress, and psychological distress were commonly associated with adverse offspring outcomes, including emotional and behavioural difficulties, neurocognitive impairments, and increased risk of psychiatric conditions from childhood to adulthood. Several primary studies reported associations independent of traditional birth outcomes, consistent with potential in-utero pathways. Plausible mechanisms included stress-related neuroendocrine processes, inflammation, and epigenetic regulation. However, heterogeneity in exposure measurement, outcome definitions, and follow-up periods limited comparability and causal interpretation.Maternal mental health during pregnancy may be an important early-life and intergenerational exposure associated with offspring developmental trajectories, but observational evidence does not establish causal in-utero transmission. Stronger causal inference, longitudinal data linkage, and incorporation of economic and policy-relevant outcomes are priorities for future research.

Abstract

Polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome (PMOS) is a prevalent endocrine-metabolic disorder characterised by follicular arrest, anovulation, and elevated androgen levels. Finally, this network-based understanding informs future research pathways, focusing on how emerging strategies-such as multi-omics integration, artificial intelligence, and stem cell secretome studies-can advance phenotype-directed precision interventions. The exposure to stressful situations is strongly related to mental diseases development. In 2009, the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approved the escitalopram for treatment of depression in children and adolescents. AIMS: To evaluate the effects of paternal chronic mild stress (CMS), with or without escitalopram treatment, on sperm chromatin protamination, testicular SIRT1 expression, reproductive outcomes, and offspring development using a rat model. METHODOLOGY: Forty-four peripubertal male rats were randomly distributed into four groups: sham-control (SC), escitalopram (E), stress (S), and stress + escitalopram (ES). Animals in the stress and stress+escitalopram groups were subjected to a CMS protocol consisting of nine randomly applied stressors over 8 weeks (postnatal Days 41-97). Escitalopram (10 mg/kg/day) was administered by gavage from postnatal Day 70 for 4 weeks. At adulthood, males were mated with healthy nulliparous females to evaluate reproductive outcomes and offspring development. Testes and epididymides were collected on postnatal Day 114 for the assessment of sperm chromatin protamination and SIRT1 immunoexpression. RESULTS: CMS and/or escitalopram treatment reduced sperm chromatin protamination, SIRT1 immunoexpression in the seminiferous epithelium, placental thickness, and increased the frequency of skeletal abnormalities in the offspring. CMS alone also reduced gravid uterus weight, litter weight, fetal weight, total placental weight, implantation, and gestational indices, while increasing preimplantation loss. CONCLUSIONS: Paternal exposure to CMS from peripuberty to adulthood was associated with impaired reproductive outcomes and offspring development, including skeletal abnormalities, reduced sperm chromatin protamination and decreased testicular SIRT1 immunoexpression. Escitalopram treatment was associated with reduced sperm chromatin protamination, decreased testicular SIRT1 immunoexpression, and an increased frequency of skeletal abnormalities in the offspring.

Intergenerational inheritance of paternal metabolic disorder: miR-29a/c-3p/glucose-6-phosphatase 1 axis in glucose intolerance propagation.
Zhao C, Lu M, Xu T, …, Gao Q
▾

Abstract

The escalating global burden of obesity and diabetes underscores the need to elucidate generational effects of parental metabolic health. This study investigates the generational inheritance of paternal obesity and glucose dysregulation in offspring, along with its underlying epigenetic mechanisms. Four-week-old male C57BL/6 J mice were randomly assigned to either a control diet (CON) or high-glucose/high-fat (HGF) group. After 4 weeks, these males were mated with healthy females to produce F1 male offspring, with the same breeding strategy applied to generate F2 males. Metabolic phenotyping revealed impaired glucose tolerance and insulin sensitivity in HGF-fed F0 males and F1/F2 male offspring, demonstrating intergenerational transmission of paternal HGF-induced metabolic dysfunction. Liver transcriptomic profiling identified elevated expression of the gluconeogenic enzyme glucose-6-phosphatase 1 (G6pc1) and reduced levels of miR-29a/c-3p in HGF males across three generations. Luciferase assay and molecular studies confirmed that G6pc1 is a direct target of miR-29a/c-3p, and miR-29a/c-3p negatively regulates G6pc1 expression. Functional analyses showed that hepatic miR-29a-3p knockdown in wild-type mice increased G6pc1 expression, inducing glucose intolerance and insulin resistance. Conversely, miR-29a-3p overexpression in HGF offspring suppressed G6pc1 and restored glucose homeostasis. Notably, sperm miR-29a/c-3p levels were consistently reduced in HGF-fed males, suggesting its role as an epigenetic carrier mediating intergenerational metabolic dysregulation. This study uncovers a paternal intergenerational inheritance mechanism whereby HGF-induced glucose dysregulation propagates intergenerationally through the miR-29a/c-3p-G6pc1 axis. These findings advance our understanding of the origins of metabolic disorders and highlight potential miRNA-targeted strategies for mitigating intergenerational metabolic risks.

Reprod Sci · 2026-10-22
The Role of RNA Interactions with PIWI in Regulating Gametogenesis, Fertilization, and Early Development Across Species.
Haddadi M, Safari-Alighiarloo N, Mikaeeli G, …, Jazayeri M
▾

Abstract

Small RNA-mediated regulatory systems maintain DNA integrity and proper functioning of the germ cell lineage. One such system consists of P-element-induced wimpy testis (PIWI) proteins and PIWI-interacting RNAs (piRNAs). Researchers initially identified piRNAs as repressors of transposable elements. Subsequent studies have identified additional roles, including regulating epigenetic programming, maintaining maternal transcript homeostasis and controlling processes involved in early embryonic development. Comparative studies of piRNAs and their functions across species have revealed substantial differences, including sex- and species-specific patterns of piRNA expression, differences in piRNA cluster organization, and functional diversity among PIWI proteins. These differences may reflect the diverse reproductive strategies and evolutionary adaptations of the species examined. Disruption of the piRNA pathway has been associated with infertility and germ-cell tumors in various studies; however, the species involved and the nature of these associations (e.g., mechanistic vs. correlative) require further investigation. These findings indicate a need for further study of piRNA regulation at the cellular level and its clinical relevance to reproductive biology. This review examines the biogenesis and biological functions of PIWI/piRNA complexes, compares PIWI/piRNA biology across species, evaluates conserved and species-specific aspects of these processes, and identifies important questions that remain underexplored in the literature.

RNA Biol · 2026-10-21
The architectural and functional landscape of transposon-derived chimeric RNAs.
Xiang Y, Liang H
▾

Abstract

This review systematically examines the architectural and functional landscape of transposable element (TE)-derived chimeric RNAs, classifying them into three major categories: TE-coding gene chimeras, TE-lncRNA chimeras and TE-TE chimeras. We delineate four potential molecular mechanisms contributing to their biogenesis: transcriptional read-through coupled with cis-splicing, promoter hijacking by TE-derived regulatory elements, trans-splicing of independent transcripts and epigenetic regulation of TE genomic loci. Functionally, TE and their chimeric RNAs can coordinately participate in genomic regulation at different levels: they remodel native gene expression patterns via alternative promoters, introduce novel functional domains into proteins and lncRNAs, and form integrated transcriptional regulatory networks. These chimeric transcripts exert critical roles in early embryonic development, stem cell potency, differentiation or pathogenic condition. Looking forward, long-read sequencing and genomic targeting technologies hold great promise to unravel the functionality and therapeutic application of TE-derived chimeric RNAs. Collectively, this review establishes TE-derived chimeric RNAs as fundamental drivers of transcriptomic innovation, evolutionary adaptation and disease aetiology, bridging basic genome biology with physiology and clinical translation.

Kyei F, Napo C, Nabawo C, …, Fosu K
▾

Abstract

Assisted Reproductive Technology (ART) has significantly improved fertility outcomes globally, yet its full potential remains underutilized. In Africa, persistent barriers including high costs, limited access, inadequate infrastructure, lack of awareness, and sociocultural and ethical concerns impedes its utility. This review explores the promising role of molecular biology and advanced molecular techniques in overcoming these challenges and advancing ART on the continent. Techniques such as preimplantation genetic testing (PGT), non-invasive prenatal testing (NIPT), CRISPR/Cas9 gene editing, mitochondrial replacement therapy (MRT), and single-cell RNA sequencing offer opportunities to enhance embryo selection, minimize genetic and epigenetic risks, and improve pregnancy success rates. Furthermore, innovations like biomarker discovery, liquid biopsy, and genomic screening can personalize treatment strategies and improve reproductive outcomes. We also provide a narrative on the progress made in expanding ART services, improving affordability through low-cost protocols, and building local expertise through training and collaborations. While challenges persist, especially in regulation and access to ART, the integration of molecular innovations in a phased manner has the potential to transform ART if implemented within appropriate infrastructural, ethical, and policy frameworks. This review underscores the need for sustained investment, policy reform, and interdisciplinary collaboration to bridge existing gaps and ensure that ART technologies are accessible, safe, and effective for all individuals and couples affected by infertility in the region.

Proc Biol Sci · 2026-10-20
Transcriptional activation of the CXCL gene family locus in mature human sperm in the presence of female-derived reproductive fluid.
Turunen T, Malinen M, Heikkinen S, …, Kekäläinen J
▾

Abstract

Mature sperm are widely considered as transcriptionally inactive cells owing to the tight compaction of their chromatin with protamine. However, sperm cells have been demonstrated to retain RNA polymerase subunits, chromatin remodellers, transcription factors, and parts of their chromatin remain in a less condensed, nucleosome-bound configuration. Consequently, some studies have suggested that sperm may be transcriptionally more active than previously assumed. Here, we tested this possibility by exposing mature human sperm to follicular fluid (FF), a bioactive liquid surrounding the oocyte. Using RNA sequencing, we first observed that FF exposure alters the sperm transcriptome. To resolve whether the observed upregulation of selected candidate genes resulted from the transcriptional activation of these genes in sperm, we labelled newly synthesized RNAs with 5-ethynyl uridine. Our results suggest that FF may trigger de novo transcription of sperm CXCL8 and CXCL3 genes and indicate that some follicular-fluid-derived transcripts are selectively transferred into sperm. Collectively, these findings suggest that specific sperm genes may become transcriptionally active in the presence of biologically relevant stimuli, potentially preparing sperm for fertilization and early embryo development. Furthermore, we envisage that activated sperm genes can have novel implications for the deeper mechanistic understanding of sexual selection.

Combined Abiotic Stress Rewires DNA Methylation to Modulate Regulatory Potential Rather Than Transcription in Arabidopsis.
Lozano-Enguita A, Morillas-Montávez A, Baca-González V, …, Caro E
▾

Abstract

Plants are simultaneously exposed to multiple abiotic stresses, yet how opposing epigenetic signals are integrated remains unclear. Here, we investigated the combined effects of heat stress and phosphate deficiency in Arabidopsis thaliana, two conditions with antagonistic impacts on DNA methylation. Using an integrated multi-omic approach (WGBS, RNA-seq and small RNA-seq) in roots and shoots under field-mimicking conditions, we show that combined stress establishes a distinct, non-additive epigenomic state characterised by widespread CHH hypomethylation and differential engagement of RdDM and CMT2 pathways. Despite extensive methylation remodelling across transposable elements, genes and intergenic regions, we observe a genome-wide decoupling between DNA methylation dynamics and transcriptional output. Notably, differentially methylated regions are enriched in transcription factor binding sites, particularly GATA motifs, linking CHH hypomethylation to increased chromatin accessibility and activation of GATA-associated regulatory networks. In contrast, nuclear mitochondrial DNA insertions represent a notable exception, where CHH hypomethylation is directly coupled to transcriptional activation. Functional analyses reveal distinct contributions of RdDM and CMT2 to basal silencing and stress responsiveness at these loci. Overall, our results support a model that positions DNA methylation as a regulator of regulatory potential rather than a direct determinant of transcription under combined stress.

▾

Abstract

Attention-deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) is a neurodevelopmental disorder characterized by inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity. Methylphenidate (MPH), a first-line medication, is often used long-term and not routinely discontinued in individuals planning to have children. Guided by the Paternal Origins of Health and Disease framework, in this study, we investigated intergenerational effects of paternal MPH exposure and reversibility of sperm epigenetic changes. Male BALB/c mice received MPH (15 mg/kg) or vehicle for 28 days and were mated with untreated females immediately after treatment (F0-T1) or after a 40-day washout period (F0-T2). The target genes of these altered miRNAs were enriched in ADHD-related pathways, including axon guidance and Rap1 signaling. After washout, the number of differentially expressed sncRNA fragments decreased to 665, whereas the number of altered miRNA-derived fragments decreased from 383 to 7. These findings suggest that paternal MPH exposure may influence offspring behavior and brain gene expression through sperm miRNA alterations and that these effects are largely reversible following drug withdrawal.

▾

Abstract

Dibutyltin dichloride (DBTCl) is a ubiquitous environmental contaminant. Prior research has established the reproductive toxicity, yet its precise impacts on embryonic developmental processes haven't been fully elucidated. The present study was designed to explore the influence of DBTCl exposure on early embryonic development in mice. This study demonstrated that exposure to DBTCl led to early embryonic developmental arrest. Collectively, these findings highlight the potential of DBTCl to inhibit early embryonic development, providing key experimental evidence for elucidating its developmental toxicity mechanisms.

Zheng Y, Qin G, Li Z, …, Zhang F
▾

Abstract

Cardiovascular disease (CVD) remains a predominant cause of global mortality. The spectrum of traditional risk factors is rapidly expanding, largely driven by the modern cardiovascular exposome. This comprehensive exposome encompasses environmental hazards, sedentary lifestyles, and severe psychosocial stress. Clinical management has evolved toward an integrated system to counteract these threats, yet traditional strategies primarily target the middle and late stages of pathogenesis. Addressing this limitation, modern research has shifted its focus to intergenerational effects. Biogerontology · 2026-10-16
Oxidative stress resistance of the extended longevity phenotype of Drosophila under paraquat exposure is heritable: influence of maternal genotype on the offspring.
Deepashree S, Shivanandappa T
▾

Abstract

Oxidative stress resistance is an important trait that contributes to the long lifespan of Drosophila melanogaster, but its epigenetic inheritance via transgenerational transmission has been poorly studied. In this study, using reciprocal crosses between normal-lifespan (NLS) and long-lifespan (LLS) lines, we investigated the transgenerational inheritance of paraquat-induced oxidative stress resistance across the parental, F1, and F2 generations, assessing survival, locomotor performance, and antioxidant biochemical defenses. The results showed that LLS flies had significantly higher resistance to paraquat than NLS flies, with females being more resistant than males. We also observed a sex-specific maternal effect on female survival, with N♂ × L♀ offspring having significantly lower mortality than L♂ × N♀ offspring in the F1 and F2 generations. Locomotor resilience was found to be the most robust transgenerational maternal effect (in both sexes and generations). Our narrow-sense heritability for negative geotaxis in males provided a measure of differential transgenerational transmission of locomotor resilience through mothers. Among the measured antioxidant defenses, glutathione was the most consistent transgenerational maternal antioxidant trait. N♂ × L♀ offspring had significantly higher glutathione levels than L♂ × N♀ in F1 and F2 generations in both sexes, with positive F2 recombination values, indicating transgenerational amplification of glutathione-based antioxidant capacity. Catalase had near-zero heritability, consistent with its predominantly nuclear regulation. Variance partitioning analysis revealed prominent maternal contributions to ROS, LPO, SOD, and locomotion in the parental generation, which were reduced in subsequent generations. Taken together, these findings indicate that the adaptive stress resistance linked with longevity is heritable in Drosophila in a trait-dependent fashion, predominantly driven by the maternal lineage. Locomotion and glutathione regulation are the most maternally inherited traits, making them the primary subjects of subsequent studies on the cytoplasmic, epigenetic, and mitochondrial pathways that mediate transgenerational stress resistance.
